A `staleDeliberatelyLocalEntries` check closes the same gap in the other direction: an allowlist entry naming a script that no longer exists. Two entries were themselves found dead rather than deliberately local and were deleted instead of allowlisted: `validate:tea-workflow-descriptions`, a byte-identical duplicate of the covered `test:tea-workflow-descriptions`, and `format:fix:staged`, whose only reason to exist was being `lint-staged`'s target before that config was inlined to call `prettier` directly; neither has any other reference anywhere in the repository. Both new checks are held by `test/test-ci-coverage.js` against synthetic input, not only by watching them pass against the already-clean repo, and `test/test-bmad-output-gated.js` pins the `_bmad/**` narrowing above the same way: each of the three configs' own extracted glob list is checked to exclude a representative `_bmad/` path and admit a representative `_bmad-output/` one.
- `validateArtifact` in `test/lib/eval-quality-inputs.js` now runs `schemaVersionProblems` beside its Ajv pass, for every kind `SCHEMA_VERSIONS` covers, and reports version problems before Ajv problems in one array. A wrong `schemaVersion` used to be a legal integer Ajv passed clean, caught only later inside `preflight`, `score` or `compile` as a generic exit-`5` `RuntimeFault` naming no TEA-level context. The check is skipped, not thrown, for the three kinds `SCHEMA_VERSIONS` does not cover (`artifact-reference`, `private-artifact-manifest`, `rubric`), so `validateArtifact` stays their Ajv-only entry point unchanged. `SCHEMA_VERSIONS` widens from the six kinds Story 2.6 derived to the three more `validateArtifact` already receives from the package, `evidence-artifact`, `sealed-evaluator-brief` and `preflight-verdict`, each read at a `validateArtifact` call site in `test/lib/probe-scoring.js` with no version check behind it until now, closing the forward reference Story 2.6 left for this.
  `eval-contract` and `scoring-policy`, the two published kinds TEA hand-authors and never ran through `validateArtifact` at all, are wired in at their one call site each. `test/test-contracts.js` runs `validateArtifact('eval-contract', source)` on every committed contract after it parses as JSON, reported and exit-worthy independently of whether `compile` throws, since the two answer different questions: version pinning against a legal Ajv value, and structural rules against a value Ajv would reject outright. `test/lib/probe-scoring.js` hoists the one `scoringPolicy()` call already made for `policy: scoringPolicy()` into a local `const policy`, so `validateArtifact('scoring-policy', policy)` checks the same value the run actually scores against, folded into the existing `schemaProblems` array `npm run test:probe-corpus` already reads.
  `validateArtifact` reads its schemas and version constants from the installed `node_modules/eval-quality` unconditionally, with no equivalent of `resolveCompiler`'s `--package` override. `test/test-contracts.js` skips the new check under `--package`, named rather than silent, because `compile` runs against the local build `--package` names while `validateArtifact` would still check every contract against a different release, which is worse than not checking: a contract wrong for the local build could report as fine, or right for it could report as broken.
  Proved the beside-Ajv wiring directly: `validateArtifact('probe', {...probe, schemaVersion: 4})` on a real probe stamped 5 returns `probe carries "schemaVersion" 4 where this build reads 5` with no Ajv error, where Ajv alone passes a wrong stamp because the field is a legal integer.
  Proved the two newly Ajv-checked kinds by seeding a one-off `schemaVersion` into `test/contracts/nfr.contract.json` and running `node test/test-contracts.js`: a new `AJV` line reports `eval-contract carries "schemaVersion" 4 where this build reads 5` and the check exits 1, independent of the seeded-fault suite's own unrelated finding on the same mutation. The same seed in `test/probes/scoring-policy.json`, run through `node test/test-probe-corpus.js`, reports `ScoringPolicyscoring-policy carries "schemaVersion" 1 where this build reads 2` against every probe in all fourteen corpora and exits 1. Both restored, every check passes.
  Proved the `--package` skip: `node test/test-contracts.js --package node_modules/eval-quality/dist/index.js` prints a named `SKIP` line and exits 0 over all fourteen contracts, where the unguarded version ran `validateArtifact` against the installed package regardless of the named build.
- The `eval-quality` pin moves from 3.0.0 to 3.1.0, the release that carries the schema-version constants, `compareDominance`, and the `eval-quality-gates` binary the plan's Epic 2 and Epic 4 stories derive from. Additive only: the full `npm test` chain passed on the moved pin with no other change.
- Every `schemaVersion` TEA writes is read from the constant `eval-quality` exports for that kind. `test/lib/eval-quality-inputs.js` stated three in a literal table under a comment conceding they were "stated rather than derived", `tools/generate-probes.js` stated the probe version and `tools/generate-contracts.js` the contract version, and the table had drifted once already, on the upgrade to 3.0.0, where its record entry read 3 against a parser that reads 6. `SCHEMA_VERSIONS` is now keyed by the published schema basename, one entry per kind TEA writes, six in all, each value the package's constant read through `require('eval-quality')`, which is stable on every Node the engines field admits and hands back the module instance `loadEvalQuality` imports. The three builders stamp through `expectedSchemaVersion`, both generators read their constant from it and export it, and `schemaVersionProblems` reports a stamp that disagrees in the package's own words. `artifact-reference` is recorded as carrying no stamp by design, with the reason, so asking for its version throws with that reason. The published schemas are unchanged, `validateArtifact` is unchanged, and every package function still goes through the asynchronous import.
  The `require('eval-quality')` reading the six constants is wrapped rather than left to throw at module load, because `test/lib/eval-quality-inputs.js` is required by callers that touch no schema version, `test/test-contracts.js` among them, which resolves `eval-quality` itself and prints a named, exit-0 skip for a tree installed with `--omit=dev`. An unguarded require reached the package before that skip ever ran, turning the documented skip into an uncaught `MODULE_NOT_FOUND` crash. A missing or broken constant is instead reported the moment `expectedSchemaVersion` is asked for a number, which also closes the gap where a renamed export read `undefined` and a caller stamped that: `JSON.stringify` drops an `undefined` value, so a generated probe or contract would have silently shipped with no `schemaVersion` field at all rather than a wrong one.
  Proved by moving `SEALED_RUN_RECORD_SCHEMA_VERSION` from 6 to 7 in the installed `dist`: `sealedRunRecord({})` stamps 7 with no TEA source edited, where the literal table kept stamping 6 and the mismatch surfaced as a fault inside `runScore`. Moving `PROBE_SCHEMA_VERSION` from 5 to 6 the same way makes `tools/generate-probes.js --check` regenerate every corpus stamped 6 against fourteen committed at 5. Proved the require-time regression by removing `node_modules/eval-quality` and running `node test/test-contracts.js`: before the fix, an uncaught `MODULE_NOT_FOUND` inside `eval-quality-inputs.js`; after, the documented `skipped: eval-quality is not installed` line and exit 0.
- The `eval-quality` 3.0.0 upgrade plan carries a linked story checklist and per-epic progress summary, reconciled against merged and open pull requests across both repositories.
- `npm run test:contracts` runs the compiler in this process instead of spawning `dist/cli/main.js` and scraping its stderr. `failureShape` reconstructed the failure code from `/^eval-quality: ([a-z-]+):/` and the issue locations from `/^\s+(\/\S*|\(root\)):/` over printed lines. `compile` throws `RuntimeFault` or `StructuralFailure` carrying `code` and `artifactPath` as fields, and a schema failure carries the Zod error as its `cause`, whose issues each carry the `path` the printed line was rendered from, so the structured channel was already there and the parse was undoing it. The process boundary goes with it, and so does the `64` usage-error branch, which existed only because a spawn can be invoked wrongly.
  Two differences are worth naming. The printed form stops at twenty issues and adds a `... and N more` line, which the baseline recorded as a `(truncated)` shape; in process there is no limit and no such shape. And `strict: true` is now stated at the call: it is `compile`'s own default and was the default of the `--strict-inputs` flag the spawn left unset, so AD-4's two strict checks run as they always did, by statement rather than by two defaults agreeing.
  `--cli <path to dist/cli/main.js>` becomes `--package <path to dist/index.js>`, which serves the same purpose of running against a local build before a release reaches npm. The whole module is imported rather than the one function, because `instanceof` is false across two copies of a package: taking `compile` from a local build and `RuntimeFault` from the installed one would report every fault as outside the compiler's declared classes. `test/contracts/expected-status.json` and `test/contracts/README.md` carry the new spelling, and the baseline is regenerated, which also sorted its thirteen keys as `--write` has always written them; the check compares per contract, so the order never affected a verdict.
  Deserialization stays outside the compile. The binary parsed at its own boundary and raised `schema-parse-failure` for a file that is not JSON, so a malformed contract was recorded as blocked with no issue list and reported by name. Putting the `JSON.parse` inside the same `try` would have classified it as a compiler that threw outside its declared classes and taken exit 2 blaming the vendor. Measured: a stray comma in `test/contracts/trace.contract.json` now reports `MOVED trace.contract.json`, observed `{"status":"blocked","code":"schema-parse-failure","issueShapes":[]}`, exit 1.

- `test/eval-trace.js` reads and writes through `eval-quality`'s shipped file-system port, certified by `npm run test:file-system-conformance` before any call site moved onto it.
  The port declares two methods and no others, `readFile` and `writeFile`, each a byte-level operation at a path: no `exists`, no `mkdir`, no `readdir`, no copy, no remove. That shape decided the conversion rather than obstructing it.
  Of the harness's 37 direct `fs` calls, 12 became port calls, 7 existence checks were deleted because the read each one guarded now answers absence itself, and 17 stay on `node:fs`. The harness carries a block naming them and separating three reasons: a directory operation the port has no method for, a file question it could answer only by reading every byte to learn a boolean, and two `copyFile` calls that a `writeBytes` this wrapper does not export would make expressible.
  An existence check that guarded a read is deleted rather than converted, which is why the harness's own hunk removes 44 lines while adding 92: the read itself answers, so asking first was the same question twice with a window in between.
  `npm run test:file-system-port` is what makes the adoption falsifiable. The conformance arm certifies the adapter and never touches a TEA call site, so a story that adopted the port and left every harness on `fs.readFileSync` would pass it unchanged, which is how Story 3.2 shipped a claim held by nothing.
  `TEA_FILE_SYSTEM_FIXTURE` hands the adapter a mechanism that answers declared paths with declared text and logs every call, and the check runs the harness under it twice: once with the ground truth scripted as text that is not JSON, which must fail the run, and once logging only, where the staged workspace's config write must appear. Both substitutions were performed and watched to fail before this was trusted.
  `test/lib/file-system-port.js` is the seam. Absence is `ENOENT` and nothing else, because a caller told "absent" about a permission error records a clean reading of a file it never read. An empty path is refused before the port sees it, since `''` passes every truthiness check and then resolves to a directory.
- The cutover's diff, measured before it landed: 7 files, 316 insertions, 62 deletions, of which 157 insertions are the new port module. `test/eval-trace.js` alone is 92 insertions against 44 deletions.
- Every remaining read of a file's contents in the eval harnesses goes through that port, so FR19's claim is now true of the suite rather than of one file. Eight harnesses and five shared readers moved. The story's inventory named three of them, the review harness, the fragment-selection harness and the probe scorer; the nfr, test-design and routing harnesses plus `eval:all` and `eval:contract-strength` were still on direct reads and are converted here too, because a closing story that leaves three behavioural harnesses reading the filesystem does not close the epic.
  The shared readers are `digestFiles` and the record write in `test/lib/eval-record.js`, `loadSuiteManifest` in `test/lib/suite-manifest.js`, and `validator` and `scoringPolicy` in `test/lib/eval-quality-inputs.js`. The last two were missing from the inventory as well, and `scoringPolicy` reads the file whose digest enters every scoring version this repository computes.
  What stays on `fs` is stated in each file it stays in: a directory walk and its guard, the lifecycle calls that create and remove a disposable workspace, the file questions the port could answer only by reading every byte to learn a boolean, and the one `statSync` whose mode bit decides whether a registered CLI carries its executable bit. `test/lib/probe-targets.js` converts nothing at all and now says so in its header, because a decline that is stated is a decision and silence reads as an oversight.
  `missingCredential` in `test/eval-test-review.js` is declined for a different reason and keeps it in the file: it asks two existence questions about files under `$HOME` and reads neither, and AD-18 keeps credential material out of anything the port logs.
  `npm run test:file-system-port` was pinned to `test/eval-trace.js` and now takes a harness and an argv, with one scripted case per converted unit: the review harness's own ground truth, the suite manifest, the fixture digest and the record write, the fragment-selection corpus and the step file its prompts quote, each of the three behavioural corpora, the menu a routing prompt is built from, every child record `eval:all` folds into its summary, a stored verdict the probe scorer reads, and the scoring policy. Thirteen substitutions were performed and watched to fail before this was trusted, one per converted reader, each restored afterwards.
  Two of them passed on the first draft, which is the part worth keeping. The digest case asserted a fixture path was in the log, and pre-flight already reads the same three fixtures to count their lines, so the path was there either way; the assertion now locates the digest's reads by where they fall, between the manifest read and the record write. The prompt readers were held by nothing at all, because `--validate-only` never assembles a prompt: the selection case now runs with `--json` and asserts the record's prompt digest moves when the step file is scripted, and the routing case scripts the menu so the corpus has nothing to route to.
  One gap is stated rather than closed. `test/eval-contract-strength.js` is exposed only through `eval:contract-strength` and `eval:preflight`, neither of which is in `npm test`, so the scripted check reaches its `stagedWorkspaceFor` (which `npm run test:probe-corpus` calls directly) and not its leg cache, its baseline read or `writeArtifact`. The file's own header says so.
  `digestFiles` keeps its `<missing>` marker, translated back from the port's absence answer, so a run that already failed because a fixture is gone still writes the record that says so. Held by a case that digests a directory with one file present and one absent and compares against the marker, and by its other half: a file the process cannot read throws rather than digesting as absent.
  One existence check was doing a second job nobody had noticed. `loadSuites` walked everything under `test/evals` and let `existsSync` filter out `suite-manifest.json`, which is a regular file, by asking about a `<file>/evals.json` that cannot exist. The port raises `ENOTDIR` there and `ENOTDIR` is not absence, so the walk now names directories only, which is where a question about a directory entry's kind belongs.
  Three bare `catch` blocks narrowed on the way through. `loadGroundTruth` in the nfr and test-design harnesses swallowed every class a read can raise and reported all of them as "missing or not valid JSON", so a permission error or a directory in place of the file told the caller about the corpus when the fault was the tree; only a `SyntaxError` reads that way now. `assertGroundTruthAbsent` in both harnesses reported a staged file that vanished between the walk and the read as an empty string, which is the same silence one file at a time, and reports it.
  Nothing moved: no stored replay result, no baseline figure, no scoring version, and all fourteen contracts still match their sources byte for byte after the asynchrony reached `tools/generate-contracts.js` through `buildPrompt`.
  The cutover's diff, insertions over deletions, per converted file: `test/lib/eval-record.js` 32/10, `test/lib/suite-manifest.js` 15/5, `test/lib/eval-quality-inputs.js` 55/15, `test/lib/probe-scoring.js` 135/61, `test/eval-test-review.js` 48/11, `test/eval-fragment-selection.js` 98/26, `test/eval-nfr.js` 108/49, `test/eval-test-design.js` 88/34, `test/eval-bmad-tea-routing.js` 78/31, `test/eval-contract-strength.js` 54/20, `test/eval-all.js` 24/9, `tools/generate-contracts.js` 63/40, and `test/test-file-system-port.js` 466/36 for the scripted cases that hold all of it.
- `finish` is asynchronous in five harnesses and awaited at every call site, because a duration now comes from a promise. `costReport` in `test/eval-contract-strength.js` takes its total elapsed time as a parameter instead of reading a clock, since it is called from a synchronous frame.
- The suite answers a peer review that found two ways a wrong document could meet every threshold. Reported here because each one changed what a green run means.
  A run could omit the most severe risk in the corpus and pass: the seeded set declares five material risks and the recall floor is 0.8, so "four of five" cleared it, and the coverage and ordering denominators shrank with the miss because both skip a risk that went unreported. `maxTopSeverityMissed` is 0, so the lowest-ranked risk in a set is reported or the run fails whatever recall comes to. Measured: deleting the unencrypted-queue risk from the correct run now fails at 4 of 5 with one top-severity miss, where it previously passed.
  A run could append four risks the fixture never anticipated and pass at 100% precision, because precision counts only the rows matching a risk the epic rules out in as many words and the seeded set declared no ceiling. `maxRisks` is now on both sets, 8 against 5 material risks on the seeded corpus. Measured: nine rows against a ceiling of eight now fails, where four invented rows previously scored clean.
- Band placement holds every risk row. It counted only the rows sitting under a heading that declared a score range, so one rangeless heading took the denominator to zero and a score-9 risk filed beneath it scored 100%. `### Medium/Low-Priority Risks` is the literal heading `test-design-qa-template.md` ships, over a table carrying both a Risk ID and a Score column. A row under a heading that declares no range is a band failure, and `test/replay/test-design/seeded-unbanded-register/` pins it at 0 of 5.
- `scoreBandOf` reads only the run of text immediately after the word `score`. Scanning the whole heading let any later pair of numbers outrank the declared bound, so `High-Priority Risks (Score >=6) - Sprint 3-4` parsed as the range 3 to 4 and reported three correctly filed risks as misfiled on a threshold of 1. It also now reads `Score 6 and above`, `Score >6`, `Critical (Score 9)` and the at-most forms, each of which previously declared no range and exempted every row beneath it.
- `parseTables` skips fenced code blocks. A table inside a fence is illustration, and the workflow's knowledge fragments and its worked example are full of them, so a run that quoted a worked register into its own document scored the example's rows as its own and hard-failed category validity, scale compliance and risk id well-formedness for quoting.
- Material risks are assigned to rows by maximum matching rather than first-come. One prose row can satisfy two declared risks, and taking the first eligible row per risk in declaration order let the earlier declaration claim it and left the later one unmatched, so a run that reported both risks correctly scored one of them.
- Every row is tested against the exclusions, including a row that already matched a material risk. Skipping matched rows rewarded compounding: one row asserting the unencrypted queue and a cross-tenant leak in the same sentence was consumed by the material pass and never tested for the risk the epic rules out, while the same content split across two rows was caught.
- Two replay documents cover four gates that had no failing case anywhere in the repository. `scaleComplianceAccuracy`, `categoryValidityAccuracy`, `riskIdWellFormedAccuracy` and `riskLinkResolutionAccuracy` all gate at 1, and stubbing all four to constants left every suite green. `seeded-shape-defects` carries a category outside the six, a probability of 7, a repeated risk id beside a malformed one, and two coverage rows citing risks the register does not declare.
- The test-design suite adopts the 3.0.0 surface the same change brought in, rather than carrying a copy of the old one. Its contract stamps `EVAL_CONTRACT_SCHEMA_VERSION` instead of a literal 4, its execution target declares `permittedEnvironmentKeys` through `environmentKeys`, its harness reads the host through `hostEnvironment(interfaceId, extraNames)`, and an operator's `--env-pass` widens that one authorization through `commandTargetPolicy`'s `environmentKeys` override.
  The environment-key omission is the one worth recording, because of how it surfaced. `permittedEnvironmentKeys` threw `target.environmentKeys is not iterable`, and the run-loop guard added earlier in this change caught it and reported a scored lost run naming the reason rather than ending the whole invocation at exit 2 with a stack trace and no result record. The guard was written for a different case and paid for itself on this one.
- A second review round closed three things the first round's fixes introduced or left open, and one silent skip the scorer had from the start.
  The new exact-band fallback parsed the template's per-risk detail headings, `### R-001: {Risk Description} (Score: 6)`, as a one-value band, which would have pinned every row of any table beneath one to a single score on a gate that sits at 1. A heading opening with a risk identifier declares no band now.
  `columnIndex`'s contains-fallback carried the bare names `ID` and `Level`, and "id" sits inside Validation, Evidence and Guidance while `Tool / Level` is the QA template's NFR header. The fallback applies to multi-word names only, which keeps the `Score (P×I)` fix and drops the substring surface.
  The ordering metric detected inversions and never flattening, so relabelling every coverage section to `### P0 (Critical)` scored 9 of 9: `higher <= lower` is satisfied by any assignment that collapses to one priority. A plan where every reported material risk reaches the same priority reports unmeasurable, which fails.
  `readRisks` required both a risk id column and a Score column and skipped a table with ids and no score without a word, so a register split across a scored table and an unscored one lost its second half silently. `maxUnscoredRiskTables` is 0.
- `--validate-only` refuses an empty token group and an empty token. An empty group holds against nothing and an empty token holds against every description, so either one makes a matcher's verdict meaningless while the case still scores full marks. Verified by construction: adding one empty token takes the corpus check from exit 0 to exit 1 naming the group.
- `test/test-eval-replay.js` pins the test-design signature function. `maxUnstableCases` cannot be breached by a stored case, because instability is a property of a pair of repetitions and a stored case is one document, so what is held instead is the function the gate is built on: a counted fixture mutation always moves the signature, and two stored designs sign identically exactly when their scored results agree. Measured on five targeted mutations, a mitigation, an owner, a requirement's wording and a test count all sign identically because none is scored, and a coverage level does not because it is. Widening the signature to reach the first four would call every repetition of a design task unstable, since an agent rewords all four on every run.
- `test/test-test-review-cli.js` resolves its `eval:all` invocations by suite id. The count of live suites was already hardened against a new suite and the two assertions beside it indexed into the manifest's order, so inserting `test-design` between `fragment-selection` and `test-review` moved index 1 onto a suite that takes neither repetition flag. That check is absent from the `npm test` chain and runs only in the sharded `cli` job, which is why it went red in CI on a green local gate.
- Corpus resolution runs through `eval-quality`'s shipped `createLocalCorpusAdapter`, certified by `npm run test:corpus-conformance` before any call site moved onto it.
  TEA digested corpora in three places and each one read the files itself: `tools/generate-probes.js` walked repository paths, `test/eval-trace.js` walked a staged workspace, and `test/lib/probe-scoring.js` digested a parsed value rather than bytes at all.
  Three readers, three ideas of what a corpus is, and no statement anywhere of what a resolver may do. The adapter states all of it: an opaque reference, a lexical escape refused before any filesystem call, the real path checked inside the root after the read, an abort honored promptly, and a mechanism answering in a shape the port does not admit treated as a fault rather than a value.
  All six published assertions pass, with the expected count read from `CONFORMANCE_OUTCOME_COUNTS`, and `test/test-port-totality.js`'s ledger moves the corpus arm from a recorded reason to the file that runs it.
  `test/lib/corpus-port.js` is the one reader for those three digests now. It resolves the members a caller names and answers synchronously from what it resolved, because the port is asynchronous and two of the three callers digest from inside synchronous builders. A reference nobody named is a named error, which is stricter than what it replaces: the old helper would digest any file in the repository, and a corpus that cannot say what it is made of is not a corpus.
  A fourth reader remains and is named rather than implied: `digestFiles` in `test/lib/eval-record.js` takes the `fixtureDigest` four live harnesses record, and it substitutes a `<missing>` marker for an absent file so a reporting path does not crash on the condition it is reporting. FR18 names the three sites this change moves; moving the fourth means deciding what the port's fault becomes in a reporting path.
  The hash stays TEA's own. Moving the bytes onto the port without moving the hash is what makes this an adoption rather than a re-attestation; `digestDirectory` is the package's own composite protocol and adopting it is a separate decision with a corpus-wide digest move behind it.
- The cutover's diff, measured against the tree it lands on rather than the one it was written against: 15 files, 687 insertions, 38 deletions, of which 401 insertions are the new corpus module and its conformance arm and the rest are the files that change behavior. No byte moved under `test/probes` or `test/contracts` beyond the corpus digest added to the strength baseline below.
- No attested corpus digest moved, and all three sites were measured rather than assumed.
  `tools/generate-probes.js` reproduces all 13 committed probe corpora byte for byte in check mode, so every `corpusDigest`, `systemDigest`, `artifactDigest`, `implementationDigest`, `commitDigest` and artifact-reference digest recorded across them is unchanged.
  `test/eval-trace.js`'s staged-workspace digest was computed both ways for both fixture sets, six members each, and matched.
  `test/lib/probe-scoring.js`'s corpus digest was computed both ways for all 13 suites and matched, because the digest input is still the contract identifier and the corpus as parsed, which is what the scorer consumes.
  Measured on the tree this ships against rather than the tree it was written against: the routing and test-design suites landed while this branch was open, taking the repository from ten corpora to thirteen, and every one of their corpora flows through the port and was measured with the rest.
  Parsed rather than raw bytes is a division of labour rather than a weaker check: `npm run test:probe-sources` regenerates every corpus and compares it byte for byte, so the bytes are held there and this digest holds what was scored.
  `test/probes/expected-strength.json` records that digest per suite now, so a change to what `suites` puts on `probes`, a sort or a stripped comment, fails the baseline instead of moving an unattested value silently.
  That split now has a gate of its own. The generator fails when a `.probes.json` under `test/probes` is one it does not write, because a corpus nobody generates would be scored here with its bytes held by nothing.
  The corpus the generator loads is three whole trees rather than named fixture directories, so a suite arriving with its own fixtures is a member the day it lands. That is not hypothetical: the routing suite arrived with `test/fixtures/tea-routing-eval` while this branch was open.
  So scores computed before and after this change are comparable on every input.
- A trace run that deletes a corpus file is reported as a mutation rather than crashing the harness.
  The digest is taken over members the port resolves, and a member it cannot resolve now returns null, which the caller reads as a mutation. Before this, the post-run read threw out of the scoring path, so a run that destroyed the benchmark took the harness down with it instead of being scored for it.

- `test-design-template.md` replaces its `## Execution Order` section with the PR / Nightly / Weekly model. The workflow's own validation checklist forbade what the template shipped: `checklist.md:102` reads "**Simple structure**: PR / Nightly / Weekly (NOT complex smoke/P0/P1/P2 tiers)" and `checklist.md:466` reads "Don't create smoke/P0/P1/P2/P3 tier structure", while the template shipped exactly a Smoke / P0 / P1 / P2-P3 tier structure, so every document produced from it failed the checklist that validates it. `steps-c/step-04-coverage-plan.md` section 3 already states the replacement and `resources/test-design-epic-3.example.md` already follows it. Found while mapping the workflow for the eval suite.
- `test-design-qa-template.md`'s four priority criteria are the epic-level template's. They made priority a function of the risk score, "High risk (>=6)", "Medium risk (3-4)" and "Low risk (1-2)", which contradicts `test-priorities-matrix.md`, `probability-impact.md`, `checklist.md` and the epic-level template, all four of which say priority is a separate judgment the score informs. The epic-level template was corrected at some point and the system-level QA template was missed. Found incidentally, and outside the eval suite's measured path, since the suite runs epic-level and never renders this template.
- Every `Action` cell under a Low-Priority risk heading now reads `Document` where it read `Monitor`. `probability-impact.md:74-77` puts 1-3 in DOCUMENT and 4-5 in MONITOR, and each of those rows sits under a heading that declares a 1-2 score band, so all four cells in the workflow that name an action named the neighbouring band's action for scores that cannot reach it: two rows in `test-design-template.md`, one in `test-design-architecture-template.md`, and one in the shipped `resources/test-design-epic-3.example.md`.
- `test/test-contract-oracles.js` reads its regex step budget from `test/probes/scoring-policy.json` rather than declaring a second one. The two had drifted: the test hardcoded 10,000 with a comment saying "the budget is never approached" while the shipped policy declares 1,000,000. The comment was true of the evidence the older contracts address, which is a JSON field holding a short string, and it stopped being true the moment a contract addressed a whole markdown document, because the estimated step count scales with the length of the value matched. The test-design oracles estimate up to 29,436 steps against a three-kilobyte document, so every one of them faulted at `budget-exhausted` in the test while scoring cleanly under the policy every real run uses.
- Scoring comparability, stated rather than assumed. No score recorded in this repository moved. `npm run test:eval-replay` reproduces every stored case with none moved, and `npm run test:probe-corpus` recomputes each probe's pre-flight, verdict, exit code, basis, qualification and strength vector against `test/probes/expected-strength.json`, where the only change is the new `nfr` entry and the `corpusDigest` field it had been missing; no existing corpus moved on any figure.
  One input is not comparable across this release, and nothing recorded depends on it. `cli/lib/parse-report.js` now reads a fence the way CommonMark defines one, so a `test-review` report using a `~~~` fence, a fence nested inside a longer fence, or CRLF line endings parses differently than it did before. A verdict measured on such a report before this release is not comparable with one measured after, because the earlier reading scored a quoted example as content on the first two shapes and stripped nothing at all on the third. No fixture, replay case or recorded measurement in this repository uses any of those three, which is why no stored verdict moved, and it is also why the change needed the fixtures this release adds.
- `maxDuplicateDomainSections`, a new nfr threshold with a ceiling of zero. `parseReport` handled a repeated `## <Domain> Assessment` heading by keeping the first section and dropping the second entirely, so a run that wrote a correct Security section and then a contradictory one scored exactly as a correct audit did, which is a gate passing for a reason other than the one its name claims. The first section still decides the domain's status, which is the answer the report leads with; what changed is that the second one is counted, carried through `scoreRun`, included in the stability signature, and printed beside the other ceilings. `step-05-generate-report.md` names a repeated section as a thing to consolidate, so this is a shape the workflow expects a run to produce, and `test/eval-trace.js` already scored the same failure mode as `maxDuplicateCriteria`. A new replay case, `test/replay/nfr/gapped-security-assessed-twice/`, is the correct gapped audit with a second Security section that reaches the opposite answer on the same two evidence files, and it differs from the correct audit in the duplicate count alone. The replay scorer version moves to 6 and every stored nfr result grew the field.
- Four probe-corpus counts the corpus outgrew, found while routing the verdict vocabularies through the new accessor and recounted against `test/probes/expected-strength.json` rather than adjusted by hand. The corpus is 55 scored probes, not 51; 33 resolve CONCERNS, not 32; 22 resolve at the Invalid rung, not 19; and 29 plan a number of legs differing from the number of checks their verdict reports, not 26. The numbers appeared in `test/lib/probe-scoring.js`, `test/eval-contract-strength.js`, `docs/explanation/eval-quality-adoption-guide.md` and in this file's own unreleased entries, each of which measured them correctly when it was written and none of which is held by anything yet.
- `test/eval-nfr.js` recorded a measurement over "all thirteen stored nfr reports" and `test/replay/nfr/` holds fifteen. The sentence went stale inside the pull request that wrote it: the measurement was taken at thirteen cases and two more landed before that branch merged, one of them `gapped-fabricated-evidence-on-a-source-label`, which exists precisely to carry a citation on a `**Source:**` label rather than an `**Evidence:**` one. So the claim that no domain-section line outside `**Evidence:**` carries a file-shaped token was true of the thirteen and is false of the fifteen. Re-measured over all fifteen: that case's `- **Source:** reports/jscpd/jscpd-report.json` is the only such line, and `gapped-report-without-sections` declares no domain section at all, so fourteen of the fifteen contribute a domain-section line. The comment now carries the original measurement as history, the re-measurement, and the reversion that reproduces it: narrowing the push back to `**Evidence:**` lines fails `nfr/gapped-fabricated-evidence-on-a-source-label` and nothing else, 1 of the 15 nfr cases, which is what says the reading itself was always held and the count was the part nothing held.
- Counts across the eval documentation that the fourteenth contract and the sixth command made stale: `test/contracts/README.md`, `test/README.md`, `README.md`, `docs/explanation/eval-quality-roadmap.md`, `docs/explanation/eval-quality-command-adapter.md` and `docs/explanation/eval-quality-adoption-guide.md` said twelve contracts, four commands, four harnesses, forty-four replay cases, and six of the eight fragment-selection skills deferred. They now say thirteen, five, five, fifty-seven, and five. The thirteenth probe corpus makes two more stale in the same files: the probe count moves from 35 to 39 and the corpus count from twelve to thirteen. The roadmap's coverage table moves `bmad-testarch-nfr` off "Fragment selection" and names what a second NFR bundle would add, and the command adapter's owed-work list strikes `nfr`. The adoption guide's layer table also gained the row for the `nfr` behavioral eval and the contract count inside it, both of which that pass had left behind.
- `test/README.md` said the trace harness "spawns the runner through `cli/lib/run-agent.js`", which stopped being true when the harnesses moved onto the command-line adapter. It probes `tea-trace-runner` through the adapter with the staged workspace as the authorization's working directory, which is what confines the run.
- The NFR contract's oracles use `containment` rather than `regex`. The workflow declares one deliverable and it is markdown, so there is no machine-readable summary to state a consequence in, and `regex`'s step estimate is `(1 + quantifiers) * length`, which an ordinary report exceeds for any "contains X anywhere" pattern. The four domain statuses, the per-domain thresholds and every evidence citation are read by the harness out of the sections that carry them; `test/contracts/README.md` records the split rather than papering it over with a JSON artifact the workflow does not declare.
- An audit of the eleven `THRESHOLDS` entries in `test/eval-nfr.js` against the stored replay corpus, written into the file beside the two entries no stored report can breach. `maxUnstableCases` compares `signatureOf` across the repetitions of one bundle and `maxFixtureMutations` compares a digest of the staged bundle before the run with the one after it, so both are properties of the run loop and a corpus holding one report and no workspace cannot exercise either. The comments say what does: the repetition loop in `main()` at `--runs 2` or more with `checkNfrSignatures` beside it, and `runCase`'s `digestTree` comparison plus its scan for added files. The third entry the audit found untested, `domainStatusAccuracy` at 0.875, is exercised by a new stored case rather than by a comment, because a report can breach it.
- `test/replay/nfr/gapped-two-domains-misjudged/`, the stored case that breaches `domainStatusAccuracy`. The bar admits one wrong judgment of the eight this corpus holds, and no stored report carried two, so the bar had never been seen failing. The run reads three moderate dependency findings as blocking and fails Security, which the bundle's stated threshold passes on 0 critical and 0 high, and reads a 1.94% monthly 5xx rate against a stated 0.5% ceiling as a caveat, which drops Reliability from FAIL to CONCERNS. Both domains are decidable, so the case rests on `domainStatusAccuracy` alone: 6 of 8 against 0.875. Both edited findings keep the threshold line they had, so threshold fidelity stays 4 of 4 and the case measures the judgment rather than the target. The headline survives the swap, because Security FAIL rolls the four statuses up to the FAIL the bundle expects, so the run publishes the right overall status having blamed the wrong domain for it.

### Fixed

- `test/eval-atdd.js`'s fixture server used a hardcoded port (4310) for the ATDD red-check harness, so two invocations of `test:probe-targets` (or `npm test`) overlapping on one host -- two worktrees, two peer sessions -- collided on "the fixture server URL is already in use" and failed several unrelated checks. Found while wiring `doc-invocations`, since that gate now runs `test:probe-targets` a second time inside `npm test`, doubling exposure to the collision. The port is now allocated from the OS once per process and reused across repetitions in that process; each run's server is fully stopped before the next starts, which was already true, so this only removes the fixed number. Confirmed reliable across two separate runs of 12-15 loops of `test:probe-targets`, one of them under real concurrent contention from another process running the same suite at the same time.
- The NFR command rejects malformed timeout values before parsing, the NFR eval records deleted evidence as a fixture mutation while preserving other read failures, and the eval documentation reports the current contract and replay counts.
- Six defects in the trace cutover, found by peer review and each verified by executing it.
  Two were mislabels the change itself introduced: an absent waiver register reported every waiver as "not a heading in" a file nobody could open, and an absent live-results file reported every declared blocker as missing from it. Both now report the absence once and stop, which takes a corpus with two missing files from eleven problems, five of them false, to four that are all true.
  A third was the duplicate underneath them: a surviving existence check reported both absences 160 lines earlier, so it was deleted and the port answers at the point of use.
  Staged workspaces leaked on every refusal path. `finish` ends in `process.exit`, which terminates without unwinding, so a call to it inside a `try` skipped the `finally` that removes the tree: nine orphaned workspaces were found in the system temporary directory. The checks record what they found, the workspace is removed, and the reporting happens after.
  `stageWorkspace` leaked on any throw between `mkdtemp` and its own `try`, a window this change had widened to include a failed adapter import and any fault the port raises. The whole body is inside it now.
  And `loadGroundTruth`'s bare catch reported a permission error, a directory, an aborted signal or a broken `eval-quality` install as "missing or not valid JSON". It catches a parse failure and rethrows the rest.
- `validateCorpus` no longer crashes on any condition it exists to report.
  A citation naming a directory or a path with a file in the middle of it threw `ENOTDIR` or `EISDIR` out of the validator, and a declared live-results file that was not JSON threw a bare `SyntaxError`. Both are problems in the report now, with the errno or the parser's message.
  The existence checks this change deleted degraded every errno to "does not exist"; the port raises all of them and distinguishes absence, so the reporting had to be written rather than inherited.
- `assertGroundTruthAbsent` reported success for a workspace it never opened.
  `filesUnder` answers with an empty list for a directory that does not exist as readily as for one that is empty, and this check is the harness's only guarantee that the agent was not handed the answers. An empty walk is a problem now, and a staged file that vanishes between the walk and the read is reported rather than contributing an empty string.
- The absence rule read one half of the fault it decides on.
  `port-boundary` wraps a mechanism's rejection in a fault whose own code says what kind of failure it was, so an abort landing while a filesystem rejection is in flight carries `ENOENT` underneath and `aborted` on top. Testing the cause alone would have handed that back as "the file is not there", which is the collapse the module exists to prevent. Both halves are tested now.

- `validateCorpus` in `test/eval-trace.js` crashed on the condition it exists to report.
  A fixture set declaring a `liveResultsFile` that does not exist threw `ENOENT` out of the validator whose entire job is reporting what is wrong with the corpus, because that read had no existence guard at all. It is reported as a problem now.
  The waiver register was the same defect wearing a different answer: an absent register reported every waiver as "not a heading in the register" rather than as a register that is not there.
  Both fell out of the port answering absence rather than a caller asking first, which is the argument for the whole change.
- `tools/validate-eval-schemas.js` compared a harness's `caseIds()` against its declared case count without awaiting it.
  That was latent until a harness read its corpus through a port: the trace harness's `caseIds` is asynchronous now, and a Promise compared against a number is never equal, so the check would have reported a case-count mismatch on a manifest that is correct. It awaits, which is the identity for the harnesses that return an array.

- The nfr corpus held each cited workflow rule to its heading existing, which is not the rule existing. A section gutted to its title passed every check, because the line span is a notice by design and a heading is cheap to keep. Each citation now declares a short verbatim fragment of the rule itself and `npm run test:eval-nfr-data` fails when the cited section no longer contains it. Measured: renaming the heading away already failed with `has no section titled`, and gutting the body while keeping the heading passed at exit 0 before and now fails naming the missing phrase. This is what answers the question of what goes red if the behaviour a status rests on is reverted, without a model call. The first version of the check did not fire at all, because it sat after a `continue` that a drifted line span reaches first, which is the same exemption-path shape it was written to close.
- An evidence gap listed as a plain bullet was counted by nothing, so on the clean bundle an invented gap scored zero false positives where the same gap in the template's checkbox shape scored one, and the threshold whose whole job is catching an invented finding carried an undeclared format dependency. A gap is now any top-level list item under the Evidence Gaps heading, named by its bolded name where it has one and by the whole item otherwise, so the ten stored results did not move and no scorer version was owed. Indented sub-bullets stay out, because the template gives each gap an owner and a deadline and counting those reports one gap as four.
- `validateCorpus` checked `thresholdSource` only when it was truthy, so an empty string satisfied the presence test and skipped the check on the one value it exists to catch. Required and non-empty now. The same shape was found four other times across three suites in one night, in a token set, a schema branch, a deciding token and a repository path.
- Four resource and classification defects in the corpus cutover, found by automated review.
  An aborted signal was relabelled a missing member, so a cancelled run would have been reported as a corpus file somebody deleted, which is the same "one code standing for every cause" the missing-member path exists to avoid. The port's own `aborted` fault propagates now, and the arm asserts it.
  A staging failure and a refused authorization each left a temporary fixture tree behind, because both throw before the caller is handed the directory its cleanup uses. Both remove it on the way out.
  The corpus arm's hanging mechanism rejected on abort, which tests the mechanism's own listener rather than the adapter's promise to settle a pending operation. It never settles and never observes the signal now, so only an adapter that answers can pass.

- Two conformance assertions that certified nothing, in the arm added here and in the command-probe arm that shipped earlier. Both `hangs` mechanisms rejected when the signal aborted, which settles the call from the mechanism's side: its abort listener is registered before the adapter's, because `runPortMethod` evaluates `mechanism(...)` as an argument and only then calls `raceAbort`, and listeners fire in registration order. So `prompt-abort` inspected a fault minted by `runPortMethod`'s `if (signal.aborted)` catch fallback rather than by the adapter's own abort handling, which is the obligation the assertion exists to certify. The two paths are distinguishable by their message, `signal was aborted before the mechanism settled` against `the signal aborted while the mechanism was in flight`, and only the second is the adapter answering. Measured against an adapter with the abort race deleted: the old mechanisms score a full pass, and a mechanism that never settles fails both `prompt-abort` outcomes with "the call had not settled 1000ms after the signal aborted". Both mechanisms now never settle, and both arms stay green against the real adapters.
- `test/test-port-totality.js` accepted `{ file: '' }` as an arm naming the check that runs it, and then threw `EISDIR` on the repository root from the process-level handler, so a typo'd path exited 1 with a stack instead of the named assertion the ledger exists to give.
- An unresolved merge conflict in `CHANGELOG.md` on `main`, shipped by the previous change and repaired here. The block sat between two bullets under `### Added`, so nothing in the gate reacted to it.
- `test/test-port-totality.js` recorded the `environment-probe` conformance arm as "the generic arm beside the command-line specialization, not yet run", which is wrong in both halves. It is the `api` arm: `dist/testing/probe-conformance.d.ts` in the published 3.0.0 names the three arms `api`, `cli` and `mcp`, its subject wants denied loopback, private, link-local and metadata addresses, an unauthorized method, an unauthorized scheme and a redirect chain, and `eval-quality/adapters` ships no HTTP adapter to run it against. TEA authorizes no HTTP target, so the arm has no subject, which is the same standing `mcp-probe` already had two lines below. It is recorded that way now, and the planning artifact withdraws the requirement with the evidence rather than leaving it as owed work.
- Two counts this change made stale, recounted from the merged tree rather than incremented.
  `README.md` said `npm test` chains twenty-five checks and it chains thirty-two once this change's own step is counted, and `docs/explanation/eval-quality-command-adapter.md` said `test:contracts` compiles twelve contracts where the repository now holds thirteen.
- Two statements in `docs/explanation/eval-quality-roadmap.md` and `docs/explanation/eval-quality-command-adapter.md` that this upgrade falsified or that were already false.
  The roadmap counted the published conformance suite at fifteen assertions, which is sixteen now.
  The adapter page said `tea-test-review` permits neither `HOME` nor `USER`, so its live pre-flight must use an API key. That stopped being true one release earlier when its request shape moved to `vendorEnvironmentNames()`, and the same page recorded the correction two hundred lines further down while the first statement stayed, so the page contradicted itself.
  The page's adoption inventory also listed `serializeArtifact` and `digestBytes` as unused, which the new corpus check falsifies, and its "done, and covered by npm test" section named none of the three checks this change adds.

- `npm run test:ci-coverage` now reconciles the CLI job's shard partition against the suites it splits, and a deliberately dropped suite number proves it fires. That job runs `test/test-test-review-cli.js` across three parallel shards by suite number, and the three lists were hardcoded in `.github/workflows/quality.yaml` with nothing holding their union to the suites the file defines. A suite added to the file and to no list is enabled in none of the three: every shard prints "skipped", all three jobs go green, and the suite runs nowhere at all. The file's own 1-to-13 bound catches a number that is not a suite and cannot catch a suite that is in no list. The check reads the suite numbers off the file's own `suiteEnabled()` call sites and fails in both directions. Found incidentally while the positional assertion below was being fixed; it is the same defect one layer up, with a worse blast radius.
- `test/test-test-review-cli.js` asserted `eval:all`'s invocation list by position, indexing `[0]` and `[1]` for the two suites that declare repetition overrides. Nothing held that order. It passed for three suites by accident of the order they were added in, and the first suite whose id sorted ahead of `fragment-selection` broke an assertion that is not about order at all. Both now look the suite up by id. Found while adding the routing suite, whose id does sort ahead of it.
- Seventeen stale counts and claims in prose, every one of which this change would have made staler. `npm test` chains twenty-five checks and the README said twenty-one; `test/contracts/` holds twelve contracts and three files said ten; `test/probes/` holds thirty-five probes across twelve corpora and two files said thirty-one across ten; the replay corpus holds forty-four cases and three files said twenty-seven; TEA ships four commands and four harnesses and five statements said three; and the README, the adoption guide and the roadmap each said that every suite had been measured live, which stops being true the moment a suite is added. These are hand-maintained counts and they will go stale again. FR23 and Story 4.1 are what end that; this is not it.
- `tea-test-review` is harder to spoof in two ways it was open to. `stripFencedCodeBlocks` in `cli/lib/parse-report.js` toggled on any line beginning with three backticks, which is not what a fence is. A `~~~` block was therefore not a fence at all, so a finding quoted inside one was read as a real finding, and a block opened with four backticks was closed by its first inner three-backtick line, so the remainder of a quoted example surfaced as content. Both are the spoof the function was written to prevent, reached by a different spelling. It now tracks fences the way CommonMark defines them: a fence opens on three or more backticks or three or more tildes and closes only on the same character, at least as long, with nothing else on the line, so a longer opener carries a shorter fence inside it and a tilde line inside a backtick block is content. Two further shapes were open and are closed with it. A carriage return is not matched by `.`, so a pattern anchored with `(.*)$` matched no line at all and stripping was silently disabled on every report written with CRLF endings. And the indent is deliberately wider than CommonMark's three spaces, because this function is a spoof defence: over-stripping costs a reader nothing and under-stripping restores the defect, and four spaces is what a fence takes inside a list item. This is a behavior change in a command that ships in this package and runs in other people's pipelines. A report that used a tilde fence, a nested fence, an indented fence or CRLF endings to carry a quoted example was scored on the example before and is not now.
- `parseReport` in `test/eval-nfr.js` scored an assessment quoted inside a fenced code block as the run's own. It walked raw lines with no idea what a fence is, and the workflow ships `resources/nfr-assessment.example.md`, a complete worked assessment with all four `## <Domain> Assessment` sections, a status for every dimension under them, twenty evidence citations and a Gate YAML snippet, so an agent that quoted the example it was shown was scored on the example. Measured on the new case `test/replay/nfr/gapped-example-quoted-in-fence/`, which is the correct gapped audit with that example quoted ahead of its own sections: before, performance FAIL, security FAIL, reliability FAIL and maintainability CONCERNS, which are the example's answers against a truth of CONCERNS, PASS, FAIL and CONCERNS, plus four duplicate domain sections and twenty fabricated citations; after, the run's own four answers, no duplicate and nothing fabricated. `cli/lib/parse-report.js` already strips fenced blocks before scanning a `test-review` report for the same spoof and `test/contracts/README.md` records it, so its `stripFencedCodeBlocks` is exported and reused here rather than reimplemented with its own opinion about what a fence is.
- `domainCoverage` counted a domain heading with no readable status as covered. `present` was "the parser made a map entry for this heading", so a section whose every status is spelled outside the four-value enum that `nfr-status-definitions.md` defines counted toward a threshold of 1 while leaving the domain Step 4 dispatched a worker for unjudged. A domain is covered when the report states a status for it, and `present` now says so. The scored `coverage` grew a second number, `sections`, which is the heading count and all the contract's own coverage oracle can see, since it reads the report as one string and `containment` cannot bind a status line to the heading above it; `nfrOracleSpecs` restates that one and `npm run test:contract-oracles` holds the two to agreeing. Measured on the new case `test/replay/nfr/gapped-maintainability-status-unreadable/`, whose Maintainability findings are all spelled `PARTIAL`: coverage was 4 of 4 before and is 3 of 4 after, with the heading count staying 4. The section still has its threshold lines, so threshold fidelity stays 4 of 4 and the case differs from `gapped-domain-omitted`, where the absent section leaves no threshold line to read. `SCORER_VERSION` moves to 7 and every stored nfr result grew `coverage.sections`; no `test-review`, `fragment-selection` or `trace` case moved.
- `signatureOf` in `test/eval-nfr.js` carried `fabricated` and nothing else about grounding, so two repetitions of one bundle that reached the same verdict off different evidence signed one string and `maxUnstableCases` counted the pair stable. Measured by construction on the stored `gapped-correct-audit` report with exactly one resolving evidence citation swapped for another resolving one: the four domain statuses, both coverage counts, the four threshold checks, the fabricated count, the evidence gap and the overall status all agree, and the two reports signed identically, byte for byte. Grounding is what varies between repetitions of a judgment that agrees, so the stability gate was blind where the risk is highest. `scoreRun` now carries `citations`, the file-shaped citations each domain's section made in report order; `signatureOf` carries it, and `projectNfrResult` in `test/test-eval-replay.js` records it, which is what lets the corpus hold the signature to it. The new case `test/replay/nfr/gapped-fault-tolerance-grounded-on-spec/` is that swap: the correct gapped audit with the Fault Tolerance dimension grounded on the retry policy in section 2.3 of the tech spec, where the correct audit grounds it on the failover drill in the observability export. Both files are in the bundle, so neither citation is fabricated, and the stored result is `gapped-correct-audit`'s in every field but the reliability citation list. That pair is what makes `checkNfrSignatures` load-bearing, executed both ways: drop `citations` from `signatureOf` and two cases with different results sign identically, drop it from `projectNfrResult` and two cases with one result sign differently, and the check fails on either. `SCORER_VERSION` moves to 8 and every stored nfr result that scores at all grew the field; no `test-review`, `fragment-selection` or `trace` case moved.
- The `overall_status` scan read the whole document, fences included, so a quoted example's own gate scalar was matched ahead of the run's. Dropping fenced lines there was not available: the Gate YAML snippet the report template ends with is itself a fenced block, so on every report the workflow produces that scalar lives inside a fence, and stripping would have left a correct report with no overall status at all. Depth is what separates them. The report's own snippet is a fence at depth 1, an example quoted into the document sits inside a longer fence so its snippet is a fence within a fence at depth 2, and a quoted example that opens no fence of its own puts its headings at depth 0 where the duplicate-section count already catches it. `parseReport` reads depth 1 and nothing else. Measured: the correct gapped audit with an example quoted ahead of it in a four-backtick fence carrying `overall_status: 'PASS'` scored `PASS` before and scores the run's own `FAIL` after.
- Ten counts and claims the `bmad-tea` routing suite left behind, found while rebasing this suite onto it and fixed here rather than filed. `docs/explanation/eval-quality-roadmap.md` still owed a behavioral suite to "the remaining eight skills" and said the machinery "measures two"; the routing suite made those seven and three, and this suite makes them six and four. Its coverage table still read "None" against `bmad-tea` and counted seven skills owed a behavioral eval where it is six. `docs/explanation/eval-quality-adoption-guide.md` had no layer-table row for the routing eval and still said `bmad-tea` has no suite of any kind. `README.md` said `npm test` chains twenty-five checks, which was twenty-seven before this change and is twenty-eight after it, beside a `test:ci-coverage` that derives the same number and prints it. The `eval:all` arithmetic in all three of those files still read 55 calls for one runner and 165 for three, and named four suites in the preflight paragraph: the routing suite's 36 intents and this suite's 4 audits make those 95, 285 and five. `docs/explanation/eval-quality-command-adapter.md` said `tea-test-review` permits `CI` on top of the vendor names, which the same release had just made false: `CI` is on no list, and `test/lib/probe-targets.js` is what says so. The same page counted eight bounded environment probes, which the routing harness's own `--version` call had already made nine and this suite's makes ten. `test/README.md` said the oracle check evaluates thirteen stored routing replies where `test/replay/bmad-tea-routing/` holds seventeen. `README.md`'s declared-gate table had no row for the `nfr` suite, which this change owed it and which the routing suite's own row shows the shape of. `package-lock.json` recorded neither `tea-routing-runner` nor `tea-nfr-runner` under `bin`.
- Two things `docs/explanation/eval-quality-command-adapter.md` and `docs/explanation/eval-quality-adoption-guide.md` had closed and did not say so. `dependency-direction` stopped running report-only in #191, and `eval-quality`'s evaluator and compiler stopped being reached by file path into `dist/` across #191 and #193, closing the page's own "two of the entry points TEA reaches are addressed by file path into `dist/`" paragraph. The command-adapter page states both now: the gate fails at zero violations, `test/test-contracts.js`'s `--package <path>` flag is gone with `npm link` in its place, and `tools/generate-lockfile-age-cache.js` queries the npm registry itself rather than eval-quality's unpublished `audit-lockfile-age.mjs`. It also gains a dedicated "The environment channel" section naming `permittedEnvironmentKeys`, `vendorEnvironmentNames()`, and why `PATH` and `CI` stay off every target's list, promoted out of a single coupling bullet where the facts were true and the symbols were not named; the adoption guide cross-links it and gains a "Stamp every artifact from the constant the package exports" section over `test/lib/eval-quality-inputs.js`'s `SCHEMA_VERSIONS` table. The page's three unscored probe classes (`test-review` gameability, `trace` and `nfr` defect, all refused under AD-9) each carry an owner and an exit condition now, matching `test/evals/suite-manifest.json`'s own deferred-entry shape; fragment-selection's `none authored` cell is stated as closed by design. `test/test-contract-oracles.js`'s own header comment made the same stale `dist/` claim about itself, corrected in place. Neither page's suite-coverage table or call-count arithmetic moved: Epic 6 is still landing suites, and every count either page states is held for a pass once it closes.
- `publish.yaml` failed at its own `npm test` step on every push-triggered run from 09:07 UTC, thirteen consecutive times, with no next-channel version shipped since (TEA Story 5.0). `test:doc-invocations` executes documented commands that need `actionlint` on `PATH` (the `ci` suite's lint half) and a working bubblewrap isolation backend (the `atdd` pre-flight path), and this job never installed either, unlike `quality.yaml`'s `validate`, `cli` and `coverage` jobs, which run the identical full chain and already carry both. Brought into line with `quality.yaml`, the same fix Story 4.8 already applied to the `coverage` job for the identical reason, with a comment recording why so it does not drift back.

## [1.26.0] - 2026-09-09

### Changed

- `tea-test-review` spent most of a small review reading files that were not in the pull request. `step-02-discover-tests.md` §2b's convention sample was capped at 40 files, and the agent runs with no shell, so each sampled file cost its own `Read` turn on every run whatever the size of the diff. The agent now reads 8. Measured on a 50-file corpus with a one-file review set: 40 files and ~233 KB before, 8 files and ~47 KB after, five times fewer reads and a fifth of the bytes. Eight clears §2b's `sampled < 4` "corpus too small to infer a house rule" floor with margin and leaves the 0.5 established/emerging ratio a denominator that means something. The sampled count is published in the report and cross-checked by `parse-report.js`, so every report states the corpus it was judged against.
- The mechanical detector scan keeps the corpus it always had. It is a separate budget from the read set: `cli/lib/convention-baseline.js` opens those files itself and pays no agent turn, so it still scans the closest 40 while the agent reads 8 of them. `mechanicalSignal: false` is what forbids a report from citing any adoption for a key, and a zero observed over 8 files is much weaker evidence of absence than a zero observed over 40. Measured on this repository before the two budgets were split, `networkFirst` went from 1 of 40 to 0 of 8, which turns `emerging` into `absent` and removes the deduction entirely.
- The 8 are spread evenly across the closest 40. Every file in the reviewed file's own directory ranks at distance 0, so among those the ranking is the alphabetical tie-break and nothing else. At a cap of 40 against a 45-file corpus that never showed; at 8 the tie-break became the selector. Measured on this repository, a review anchored in `test/` sampled `eval-all.js` through `test-contracts.js` and nothing named `test-e*` or later was reachable by any review anchored there, on any run. Taking every `floor(i × 40 / 8)`-th file keeps the count, the determinism and the neighborhood, and the sample now spans `test/`, `test/lib/`, `test/schema/` and `test/fixtures/`.
- The convention corpus counts a file only when its name says it is a test, and skips `fixtures`, `__fixtures__`, `__mocks__` and `testdata` trees. `isTestFile` is deliberately wider, because the review set has to catch a changed helper under a test directory, and a directory-only match was never right for a baseline: on this repository it admitted `stub-agent.js` and a React component under `trace-eval/clean/src/tokens/`, and it admitted the seeded-defect specs under `test/fixtures/test-review-eval/seeded/` that the evals score against, so the house convention was partly established from deliberate anti-patterns. This repository's own corpus is now empty and its self-review reports the baseline as unavailable, which is the truthful answer for a repository whose only spec files are eval fixtures.
- The four quality workers in `step-03-quality-evaluation.md` can now actually run in parallel. Three things had to change together. `cli/lib/build-prompt.js` pinned `tea_execution_mode=sequential`; the `claude` adapter's tool list carried no delegation tool, so step-03's capability probe found no launcher; and `tea_capability_probe` was never stated at all, which resolves `auto` to `sequential` on every run whatever the mode says. The prompt now states both orchestration keys, and `cli/lib/agent-adapters.js` grants `Task` from the `scoped-artifact-writes` tier upward in both `--tools` and `--allowedTools`. A launched subagent inherits that list exactly, verified live against claude 2.1.266: the child reported `Agent, Edit, Glob, Grep, Read, Write` and no shell, so "the shell only under command-execution" holds at the workers too. `read-only` runners get no delegation, since the workers' declared outputs cannot exist at that tier, and `codex exec` exposes no subagent launcher, so `auto` resolves to `sequential` there and that adapter's run is unchanged.
- `tea_execution_mode` and `tea_capability_probe` resolve through the same chain the fragment keys do: `--execution-mode` / `--capability-probe` / `--no-capability-probe`, then `_bmad/tea/config.yaml`, then `src/module.yaml`. Both were previously stated in the prompt as literals, and the prompt declares that stated values beat `config.yaml`, so `tea_execution_mode: sequential` in the file that `docs/reference/troubleshooting.md` tells users to edit was being ignored.
- The report carries a `**Execution Mode**:` line and the verdict JSON carries `executionMode`, required on any run that measured a convention baseline. step-03 resolves its mode from a runtime capability probe and printed the result only to the agent's stdout, which the CLI reads only when the report is missing, so a run that asked for parallel workers and silently got `sequential` was indistinguishable afterwards from one that got what it asked for. `step-03f-aggregate-scores.md` also stopped hardcoding `subagent_execution: 'PARALLEL (4 quality dimensions)'` and `performance_gain: '~60% faster than sequential'` regardless of what ran.
- The CLI mints a `tea_run_id` and the workflow uses it as the timestamp in its four worker output paths. step-03 asked the agent to generate one with `new Date().toISOString()`, which an agent with no clock and no shell cannot execute, so it emitted a plausible string; nothing cleans `/tmp/tea-test-review-*` and step-03 checks only that the four files exist, so two runs landing on the same invented value would aggregate each other's scores.
- The default agent wall-clock timeout scales with the review set: 20 minutes plus 2 minutes per reviewed file, capped at the 30 minutes it used to be flat at, with `--timeout-ms` still overriding it. Nothing bounds how many turns the agent takes and no supported vendor CLI offers a turn cap (`claude` 2.1.266 has `--max-budget-usd` and no `--max-turns`; `codex exec` has neither), so the wall clock is the only vendor-agnostic bound. A one-file review that stopped making progress used to be indistinguishable from a slow one for half an hour.

### Fixed

- Three statements of the module defaults that disagreed with `src/module.yaml` and with `cli/lib/resolve-tea-config.js`'s `MODULE_DEFAULTS`, which a test already holds equal to each other. `--use-pactjs-utils`'s help said the default is `false` and `--pact-mcp`'s said `none`; both are `true` and `"mcp"`. `docs/reference/tea-test-review-cli.md` repeated the same two wrong values and then advised the opposite of what the CLI does, telling a contract-testing repo it gets `tea_use_pactjs_utils: false` by default and should pass `--use-pactjs-utils` to change it. A repo on raw `@pact-foundation/pact` needs `--no-use-pactjs-utils`.

## [1.25.2] - 2026-09-09

### Fixed

- `trace`'s clean control scored FAIL at exit 2 because five of its twenty-six oracles abstained. `O-009`, `O-010`, `O-011`, `O-013` and `O-014` are `for-any` quantifiers over the seeded set's summary, and `P-004`'s record carried one observation that both plan steps selected, so those five quantified over the clean summary's empty or absent collections. Two changes close it and they work only together: each plan step binds `stdin.prompt` as a literal of that set's own prompt, and the clean control's record carries both fixture sets' runs. Measured on the stored replay, `P-004` moves from FAIL at exit 2 with five abstentions to CONCERNS at exit 0 with all twenty-six oracles resolving `passed-clean-control`; four unsatisfied AD-20 coverage rules hold it at CONCERNS and are separate work. Either half alone is worse than neither, and both were run before this was accepted: two observations under the old matcher bindings leave every observation satisfying both steps, so `exactly-one` reports selector ambiguity on all twenty-six oracles at exit 3; literals over one observation select nothing, so all twenty-six resolve `unreached` and the run reports CONCERNS at exit 0 having examined no evidence, which is a silent green worse than the FAIL it replaces. A literal is compared with `deepEquals`, so the contract's literal and the record's prompt are both `buildPrompt` from `test/eval-trace.js`, and `traceEvidence` throws when the contract on disk binds any other bytes. The record used to carry the placeholder `The prompt the trace harness assembles for <case>.`, which is a prompt no run has ever sent. The control still catches a false positive: a clean summary damaged to derive FAIL with an invented blocker, reported as a defect finding, resolves `O-016` to `false-positive` and the probe to FAIL at exit 2. `test/contracts/trace.contract.json` grows 3.6 kilobytes, the size of the two prompts; the eight fragment-selection contracts keep the matcher binding, where the same fix costs 42 to 143 kilobytes each and roughly doubles every file.
- A stale reason in `test/contracts/README.md`, surfaced by the contradiction the entry above created. The paragraph on `trace.contract.json`'s witness said its two plan steps share one prompt on purpose because `test/eval-trace.js` names no fact about either fixture set in it, and it said the witness legs are runnable only against a staged workspace of the seeded set. Both stopped being true one release earlier: each set declares its own `projectRoot`, the whole prompt is written against it, and both witness legs stage the clean set. The `allow_gate` reasoning, the differential-and-invariance argument, and the pointer to `testData.setup` all survive and are kept. `testData.setup` in the contract itself was already correct and needed no change.
- A stale illustration in `docs/explanation/eval-quality-command-adapter.md`. The passage on AD-4 said `trace`'s five `for-any` oracles over the seeded export "still abstain on a clean run", which stopped being true once each plan step selected its own set's run. AD-4's rule is unchanged and the sentence now dates the abstention it describes.

## [1.25.1] - 2026-09-09

### Changed

- `eval-quality` moves from 1.3.0 to 1.4.0, which closes three findings this repository raised against it, and the probe corpora record what changed. Measured on the stored replay across the two versions: `test-review`'s five plants that had failed pre-flight on `seeded-faults-scoped` with a null verdict and exit 3 now pass pre-flight and score, taking the suite's defect class from four exercised and four caught to nine and nine at a rate of 1; `trace`'s O-023 and O-024 move from `abstained` to `passed-clean-control`, because a bare `count-tolerance` with `expected: 0` now counts a collection observed to be present and empty; and `trace`'s three defect probes report `condition-artifact-channel-contract-local`, the reason AD-9's gate refused them, where before a rejected probe surfaced only as `infrastructure-error` and exit 3. `expected-strength.json` records a `qualification` field per probe, so a rejection that changes its reason shows up as a diff.
- Each trace fixture set is staged under its own project root, declared as `projectRoot` in `test/fixtures/trace-eval/ground-truth.json` and named for the epic the set traces: `tenant-data-export` and `api-token-lifecycle`. Both sets used to stage at one `project/` root and `buildPrompt` read nothing off the set it was given, so the two sets sent byte-identical prompts and the staged workspace, which is the run's real input, was nameable from no request. The whole prompt is written against the set's root now, so a leg can ask for the set it means: `stagedWorkspaceFor` stages the set the prompt names, the stored-evidence port answers with that set's stored run, and the stub agent resolves `{project-root}` off the prompt the way a real agent does. `validateCorpus` fails a corpus whose two sets share a root or whose root names the set's role, because a run that read `clean` in the directory it works in would have been handed the answer.

### Fixed

- Four passages describing limitations that no longer exist. `test/probes/README.md` and `docs/explanation/eval-quality-command-adapter.md` each said a rejected probe carries no reason across the boundary; the adapter document also said "this collection is empty" has no spelling, and argued that no leg TEA could add repairs `seeded-faults-scoped` firing on a leg carrying the fault leg's own request. All four are rewritten against what 1.4.0 does, with the measured before and after. One finding stays open and belongs to TEA rather than to `eval-quality`: `trace`'s three defect probes address a file the command wrote, which the qualification gate refuses by design. The other is closed below.
- `trace`'s three defect probes failed pre-flight on `seeded-faults-scoped`, reported as `D-001: the manifestation witness fires on clean leg "witness-gate-withheld"`. The witness was right and the leg was not clean. AD-10 reads every other leg of an operation as a clean leg, `trace-fixture-set` declares `stateChangeMarker: true` so no control leg is planned for it, and the only other legs it had were its two sensitivity witness legs, both staging the seeded set. The manifestation witness asserts a coverage number the seeded workspace produces and `allow_gate` does not move, so it fired there truthfully.
